Text:"CoworkOS: Scaling Agent Coworkers for Real-world AutomationRapid advances in cowork agents are shifting human-AI collaboration toward real-world automation, delivering software, designs, and other artifacts. However, such productivity work demands both high-quality delivery on complex productivity tasks and continued monitoring and decision-making over long horizons, combination that remains an open challenge. We introduce COWORKOS, general-purpose harness for long-horizon work that separates task strategy from execution management: the model decides actions, delegation, and acceptance, while the runtime maintains state and feedback. Its three-layer architecture enables direct execution or delegation to Subagents, synchronously or asynchronously, under stable responsibilities. Through shared application layer, humans and agents inspect and revise the same evolving documents, presentations, CAD models, and Blender scenes, so human-in-the-loop participation can enter both through conversation and through direct modification of the work itself. To sustain continuity across agents and sessions, COWORKOS further introduces hierarchical workspaces and persistent semantic memory, so working files, key decisions, and long-running Job logs remain shareable, reusable, and resumable after interruption. Across 12 benchmarks and 6 categories of real-world applications, COWORKOS proves highly competitive with frontier agent applications such as Codex and DeepSeek Harness. As sustained-work case study, COWORKOS uses GLM-5.3 as its backbone to conduct RSI-driven automated post-training on Qwen3.6-35B-A3B. Over five rounds, the models average score across eight benchmarks rose from 33.19 to 42.95 (+9.76). Looking ahead, we believe COWORKOS opens meaningful path toward general cowork-agent intelligence. (cid:231) Project Page: coworkos-ai.github.io (cid:97) Repo Link: github.com/RUC-NLPIR/CoworkOS (cid:535) Demo Gallery: coworkos-ai.github.io/showcase.html (cid:263) Contact: jinjiajie@ruc.edu.cn, tgui@fudan.edu.cn, dou@ruc.edu.cn Figure 1: Overview of 24 application cases produced with COWORKOS across six categories: frontend websites, games, Office presentations, interactive visualizations, CAD, and Blender rendering. Two views of each case illustrate different pages, scenes, or inspection states. The montage provides visual overview; harness comparisons and detailed inspection evidence follow in Figure 6 and Appendix A.2.Agent intelligence is advancing toward lifelong autonomy systems that continually interact and evolve with real-world environments. Progress toward Artificial Super Intelligence (ASI) requires attention to both model capability and the runtime systems that translate reasoning into action. Ideally, such agents would invoke tools, interpret feedback, maintain working state, and continue when their immediate interaction ends. To achieve this, the agent harness supplies the runtime mechanisms for this process, including tool orchestration, state and context management, and execution feedback. Because the harness shapes how model acts and how well it can use available resources, models and their harnesses need to co-evolve (Liu et al., 2024; Sumers et al., 2024). Recently, the evolution of agent systems can be understood through three progressive stages. Agent Workflow centers on reasoning and tool-using trajectories: early mechanisms include Chain-of-Thought and Tree-of-Thoughts (Wei et al., 2022; Yao et al., 2023a), interleaved reasoning and action in ReAct (Yao et al., 2023b), and feedback-driven reflection in Reflexion (Shinn et al., 2023). Workflow frameworks and construction methods, including DSPy (Khattab et al., 2023), AFlow (Zhang et al., 2024), and MetaGPT (Hong et al., 2024), further organize these capabilities. Agent Assistant extends this scope to persistent individual needs, bringing memory, messaging, code execution, and tool interfaces into continuing interactions. Frameworks such as LangChain, LangGraph (LangChain, 2026), AutoGen (Wu et al., 2023), and OpenClaw support different aspects of this development. The emerging Agent Cowork setting places agents within professional work: people and agents contribute to evolving artifacts, adjust their plans as work develops, and share initiative in complex productivity tasks. Coding and work-environment agents represented by Claude Code, Codex, OpenHands, and DeepSeek Harness have demonstrated strong real-world productivity (Anthropic, 2026; DeepSeek AI, 2026; Jimenez et al., 2024; OpenAI, 2026; Wang et al., 2025), while cowork agents such as Kimi Code, ZCode, Qoder, and Hermes Agent have also been widely adopted. Notably, these stages describe an expanding design lens: from automation by the agent itself, gradually toward sustained interactive work involving humans, agents, and the environment (Liu et al., 2024; Xie et al., 2024). Building on this insight, we argue that this expansion of productivity reveals two complementary requirements for todays cowork agents, illustrated in Figure 2(a): Complex productivity tasks: producing software, documents, designs, and other artifacts through demanding reasoning, tool use, and verification. Such tasks call for the agents comprehensive agentic intelligence and, where the work requires it, collective intelligence across collaborating agents, to carry difficult task through to completion. Continued monitoring and decision-making: monitoring training process over days, following project across sessions, or watching external information and deciding whether and how to act when relevant event occurs. Such monitoring requires the agent to keep its context healthy over time, maintain sound memory, and continuously manage and track the artifacts it produces. Notably, both settings require sustained collaboration between humans and agents, which we treat as basic design principle for cowork agents: the two sides work together within shared application ecosystem centered on evolving work (Figure 2(b)). This leads to our central question: can single cowork agent handle both complex productivity tasks and continued monitoring and decision-making, while keeping humans in the loop? We propose COWORKOS, cowork harness for long-horizon and human-in-the-loop task execution. To achieve this, natural approach is division of responsibility: the model autonomously chooses the task-execution strategy, while the runtime harness maintains efficient, long-horizon cowork mechanisms. Specifically, COWORKO"

Text:"CUA-Master: Scaling Computer-use Agents through Open-World Recursive Improvements Xinlei Yu, Gen Li, Ji Zhao, Zhangquan Chen, Congcong Wang, Shuai Dong, Zelin Peng, Yuqi Xu, Hongrui Wu, Kaiwen Tuo, Cheng Yang, Kaituo Feng, Yifan Wu, Jiayi Zhang, Dongming Wu, Wenqi Shao, Jiaqi Wang, Xiangyu YueQ MMLab, CUHK THU HKUST(GZ) Explore Academy, JD (cid:140) Project (cid:135) Code Abstract Despite recent advances in CUAs, their development remains constrained by the limited diversity, controllability, and reliability of interaction data. Moreover, conventional optimization pipelines lack iterative feedback, while existing recursive improvement approaches often rely on self-generated experiences, limiting ability expansion and yielding diminishing returns. To address these challenges, we introduce CUA-Master, nested double-loop recursive improvement framework. At the system level, an outer world-data-model flywheel continuously incorporates open-world corpus and leverages evolving model weaknesses to guide subsequent data construction and optimization iterations. At the data level, an inner flywheel couples open-world seeding and autocatalytic propagation. The former mines authentic workflows as seed tasks from instructional videos, and employs visual-semantic dual-path trajectory reconstruction; The latter transforms these seeds into reusable primitives for controllable synthesis of diverse, complex, and verifiable tasks. To enable efficient and reliable trajectory collection, we further introduce feedback-guided snapshot-rollback retry mechanism in various real environments. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CUA-Master-27B achieves competitive performance across GUI-only and GUI-CLI hybrid benchmarks, scoring 80.2 on OSWorld Verified, 36.9 on OSWorld 2.0, 43.9 on WeaveBench, and 25.0 on Agents Last Exam. Our framework also substantially improves the quality and efficiency of data synthesis, and exhibits continuous gains across recursive iterations. Figure 1 Performance"

Text:". t e fi o s e W . i i l W C r e c a n r x g r s e e - t m g g h b T . L , . g f Z i a n S , T U r N p R i h A C o - p e i q r w w f e p o s t a d v i o t e s - u m n l m t d t e n , U r N s r . e t o l m t n i e c n t h n l w l n i x s . a a c r p g o s n e u - u o n r c r r , o c e i o n t s i , e a U t N t s a l t e e e d i p - e t u t r e s t s e w F T . m u k s c d a , i c s o r - d a i s a u e l t e o p - e i r r , s d l n o . e t e t e fi i o n r L g S . r o e l n d b t t a a o n t i d fi e e e g r e e p c G l e t e y e m f a n n r t e e e e h - a r m s r n h i o T . i t - fi s e u z r - o a n a t - p l t i l , n e n e o i b o k t i g o s e H m h n p e P n , i , N i u r T . m v m y o d o t o p e r e e e - d , i u g G o a e m r s - o n s l r r - t - - a e e a l f r o . ) 1 u F ( n e n a l d t e e e n i c t i d a , t e p d n - o i o c e o c c i r p a r U - C o , fl r e e n p - a h , t g . ff e t c a t a d u e g r ff , N R - n o a n r n t n f b u a a i f d g m f p - i a U r N l a s c . o l m t n i o r w w f e p y r a t e a R S s n - U r N / f z i - h / . h g o r - h o / a - e / . c n g s e / u r n / b / . p e a 1 . e g i l t e a c c r f o P - h o . u F NorthCUA 1 IntroductionComputer-use agents (CUAs) extend the capabilities of large language models from generating responses to operating computers and automating software tasks (Zhang et al., 2025b, Wang et al., 2025b). They can invoke software functions through application programming interfaces (APIs) and command-line interfaces (CLIs). Graphical user interfaces (GUIs) extend this access to functions that applications do not expose programmatically, providing critical last mile for software automation (Zhang et al., 2025a,c). Through GUI interaction, agents interpret the screen and translate natural-language goals into mouse and keyboard actions. Advances in visual grounding and action generation have expanded the range of operations these agents can perform (Qin et al., 2025, Wang et al., 2025c, Zhou et al., 2025). As agents combine these operations into longer workflows, earlier actions shape the software state on which later decisions depend. Reliable completion requires agents to preserve intermediate progress and the users constraints, adapt to changing interfaces, and recover from errors while keeping the overall goal in view (Zhang et al., 2026, Lu et al., 2026). Training agents to coordinate such workflows therefore requires experience that develops precise interface skills and connects successive decisions to complete task outcomes. Obtaining this experience at scale begins with constructing varied tasks, each of which needs request grounded in available software and content, usable initial environment, and reliable way to judge success (Ge et al., 2026, Zhou et al., 2026b). Once these tasks are available, collecting experience from them requires an agent framework that sustains long interactions and records decisions and outcomes across desktop and web environments. The resulting demonstrations can teach the policy how to complete workflows, but independent execution may lead it into states absent from those demonstrations. Continued improvement therefore requires fresh interaction and outcome feedback from the policys own attempts (Wang et al., 2025c, Jung et al., 2026, Lu et al., 2026). Meeting these linked needs calls for co-designing how tasks are constructed, how agents execute them, and how the resulting experience is used for learning. We present NorthCUA, an integrated system for turbocharging computer-use agents through experience at scale. Our central idea is to co-design the production and use of experience, making software interaction continuing source of learning. We connect executable tasks to policy updates through three components (Figure 2), focusing on GUI-based software operation. The Experience Flywheel constructs executable tasks for the NorthCUA Harness to run and assess. The recorded trajectories and outcome evidence guide curation, supplying Staged Learning with demonstrations that complement the Flywheels separately constructed grounding examples. This mixture develops interface understanding and workflow completion. Shared execution and outcome interfaces also let the learner collect fresh attempts from the policy itself, extending the same system from demonstration collection to continued policy improvement. full-lifecycle infrastructure. We design the NorthCUA Harness as fullNorthCUA Harness: lifecycle infrastructure that executes software tasks and turns the resulting attempts into inspectable experience for learning. This requires keeping the agents decisions connected to its workspace and outcomes, from environment preparation through execution and evaluation to record preservation. Within the shared runtime, visual context and task memory help agents build on progress across long workflows (Zhang et al., 2026, 2025d). An evaluator uses the execution record and live workspace to check task completion and execution quality, supplementing task-specific verifiers where their coverage is incomplete (Zhuge et al., NorthCUA Technical Report 2 / NorthCUA 1 Introduction Figure 2. The NorthCUA experience loop. 01 The harness executes desktop and web tasks, records their trajectories, and retains workspaces through required live inspection. 02 The Experience Flywheel expands intents, environments, and verifiers into executable tasks, then curates assessed trajectories. Text:"FLAMIA: FLAsh MIcroscaling Attention for Fast and Causally Safe Training on Blackwell Fedor Velikonivtsev, Mikhail Koltakov, Denis KuznedelevAttention remains substantial computational cost in Transformer training, particularly at long context lengths. NVIDIA Blackwell GPUs provide high-throughput low-precision matrix multiplication, but exploiting it in attention requires addressing quantization overhead, causal information leakage through shared scales, and numerical inconsistencies between the forward and backward passes. We present FLAMIA, family of attention kernels for Blackwell with MXFP8 and mixed NVFP4/FP8 training paths. Our design combines token-block scaling that reuses quantized operands across matrix orientations, targeted safeguards against scale-induced leakage, and consistent forward and backward computations. Fused quantization and architecture-specific kernel optimizations reduce overhead, while document-local scaling and workload-aware scheduling support variable-length documents directly in packed layouts without repacking. Across various causal packed-document workloads, the MXFP8 path accelerates the combined forward and backward attention call, including quantization, by factors of 1.101.40 on B200 and 1.061.42 on B300 relative to the fastest evaluated baseline for each workload. We validate training stability and quality preservation on pretraining Llama-style models. These results show how jointly designing quantization, training numerics, and document scheduling translates low-bit hardware throughput into practical attention acceleration. 1. Introduction Softmax attention [1] remains substantial cost in Transformer training and inference, particularly at long context lengths. FlashAttention [2] reduces memory traffic through tiled computation and online softmax. FlashAttention-4 [3] further optimizes attention for NVIDIA Blackwell GPUs, but its BF16 implementation leaves higher low-precision throughput unexploited: Blackwell tensor cores support FP8 and FP4 matrix multiplication at twice and four times the BF16 rate, respectively [4]. Realizing these gains requires addressing quantization overhead and training numerics, while supporting the variable-length documents packed together during pretraining. Recent 4-bit extensions of FlashAttention-4 [5, 6] motivate examining these requirements beyond single-step accuracy. Three constraints are central. First, hardware block scaling follows the contraction axis, whereas attention training uses operands along different contraction axes, potentially requiring separately quantized representations. Second, scales shared across positions can let future tokens affect earlier outputs despite causal mask. Third, the backward pass must consistently reconstruct the forwards quantized intermediates: mismatches in quantization, tiling, or exponential evaluation can destabilize training despite high single-step cosine similarity. Packed inputs additionally require document-local scaling and load balancing. Among the evaluated implementations, some provide only 1 forward path for packed inputs [7], use tensor-wide FP8 scaling [8], or lack causal-mask support [9]. We present FLAMIA (FLAsh MIcroscaling Attention), low-precision attention kernels for Blackwell with MXFP8 and mixed NVFP4/FP8 training paths. The mixed path uses NVFP4 for QK and its recomputation, and 8-bit operands for the remaining matrix multiplications. Token-block scaling enables reuse of 8-bit representations across orientations. FLAMIA combines targeted leakage mitigations with consistent forward and backward computations and processes packed documents without repacking. Default scaling retains within-block dependencies and sequence-wide backward accumulator dependency. Exact options remove them in supported batched causal calls. Our contributions are: Two training paths using token-block scaling to reduce quantized representation duplication across matrix orientations. An analysis of scale-induced leakage and forwardbackward inconsistencies, with targeted mitigations and training experiments showing that single-step accuracy proves neither causal correctness nor training stability. Kernels combining fused quantization, coordinated softmax and matrix-multiplication scheduling, low-precision two-CTA backward pass, and GPU-based scheduling for packed documents, plus two bit-level exponential approximations for inference. An evaluation against FlashAttention-4, cuDNN, FlashInfer, Low Precision FA4 [9], and the Hardware-Aware FP4 FlashAttention-4 (HW-FP4 FA4) of Hu [5]. Across all evaluated causal packed-document workloads, MXFP8 achieves the lowest combined forward and backward attention latency, including quantization: 1.101.40 speedups on B200 and 1.061.42 on B300 over the fastest evaluated baseline per workload. In 1B-model pretraining on 100B tokens, MXFP8 and mixed NVFP4/FP8 lead only to marginal increase of training loss and change of downstream metrics. The code is available at https://github.com/yandex-research/flamia-blackwell-atten tion. 2. Background and Related Work FlashAttention Let Q, K, RLd be the query, key, and value tensors corresponding to single head, with sequence length and head dimension d. The attention output is computed as follows: RLL, = QK/ = softmax(S) RLL, = PV RLd.dV = PdO, dP = dO V, dS = (dP D), dQ = dS K, dK = dSQ, with the row sums Di = (cid:80) Pij dPij, so every operand takes part in two products, contracted along different axes. naive implementation materializes and in global device memory, resulting in large memory footprint and substantial I/O traffic. FlashAttention [2] combines tiled computation with online softmax to keep intermediate tiles in fast on-chip memory. FlashAttention-2 [10] reduces non-matmul operations and improves work partitioning, while 2 FlashAttention-3 [11] and FlashAttention-4 [3] introduce optimizations tailored to NVIDIA Hopper and Blackwell GPUs, respectively. In our work, we focus on further improving attention performance on Blackwell, using BF16 FlashAttention-4 as starting point. Microscaling floating-point formats Microscaling formats [12] employ fine-grained quantization in which the elements of block share common scaling factor: each element is stored as low-bit code, and its value is the code times the blocks scaling factor. We call scaling factor scale for short. NVIDIA Blackwell provides native hardware support for these formats [4]. MXFP formats (MXFP4, MXFP6, and MXFP8) use bloc"

Existing video world models leave 3D dynamics implicit, action-based formulations remain tied to specific robot configurations, and 3D motion prediction models use flow-only supervision, providing limited learning signal for physical dynamics. To circumvent these limitations, we introduce WorldFlow, generative world model that jointly predicts future video and query-based 3D trajectories. By parameterizing 3D motion as continuous displacement tokens aligned with the video grid, unified Mixture-of-Transformers (MoT) backbone jointly denoises both modalities under rectified-flow objective. This formulation couples explicit geometric dynamics with visual evolution, effectively unlocking the physical commonsense embedded within pretrained video models. To empirically scale and validate this unified architecture, we train WorldFlow on heterogeneous mixture of 1.44 million object-level samples spanning simulated and real-world domains. We further introduce WorldFlowBench, comprising 1,678 evaluation samples from 11 data sources across simulated and real-world interactions, including spatial reasoning tasks. Across all three evaluation sets, WorldFlow achieves the lowest average displacement error (ADE) and final displacement error (FDE) among the evaluated learning-based methods. Notably, on the challenging SpatialCog set, WorldFlow achieves an ADE of 0.060 m, 54.2% reduction compared with the strongest external baseline. It also reduces ADE and FDE by 55.9% and 68.5%, respectively, compared with its flow-only variant. Project page: https://worldflow-rbs.github.io/. Figure 1. 3D Flow Prediction"

Text:"Can Large Language Model Agents Learn by Watching Tutorial Videos? Jun Wang Haoyu Zhao Zafeirios Fountas University College London Humans continuously acquire knowledge from carefully crafted materials such as books and tutorial videos, carrying that understanding into future tasks. Millions of such instructional videos are uploaded online every day, yet LLM agents remain unable to exploit this ever-expanding wealth of human teaching. Their capabilities are frozen after training, while parameter updates at scale remain prohibitively expensive. This raises the central question: can LLM agents learn from this vast instructional resource without parameter updates? We introduce Tutorial Agentic Learning (TAL), nonparametric continual-learning paradigm that consolidates tutorial videos into persistent, structured tutorialbook memory through two-stage adversarial process that harvests hard, video-grounded questions and uses them to refine the memory. At inference time, TAL retrieves through Table-of-Contents (TOC) tree, tying retrieval directly to the models reasoning rather than to embedding similarity. To study this setting, we introduce VidLearn, multimodal QA benchmark built from 72 chess tutorial videos spanning 12 openings. On VidLearn, TAL outperforms all evaluated baselines and improves monotonically as more videos are consumed, achieving up to 113% and 91% relative gains over the corresponding vanilla MLLMs on the training and test splits, respectively. In actual gameplay, TAL reaches stronger opening-phase position than its vanilla counterpart in 69% of head-to-head games. Evaluation in second domain further demonstrates the generality of TAL. Together, these results highlight instructional videos as scalable resource for parameter-update-free agent continual learning. 1. Each quality score is used directly as MAD decision score. Unlike prior work, which inverted quality measure when this lowered its error, we fix the score direction of each method in advance from its definition, so that cases in which morphs receive higher quality than bona fide samples remain visible. On FRLL, DifFIQA(R) achieves mean D-EER of 11.42%, and together with CLIB-FIQA and eDifFIQA(L) clearly outperforms all other FIQA methods. strong separation of bona fide and morph scores does not imply good detection performance: on the diffusion-based attacks, up to 11 of 17 methods assign higher quality to morphs than to bona fide samples, and the largest reversed separation in the study coincides with the worst detection result of the study. Performance also depends strongly on the attack: GraFIQs(L), the best detector of DCMorph and Arc2Morph on FRLL, performs poorly on the landmark-based and GAN-based attacks. On FEI, the lowest mean D-EER is 39.75%, and threshold set on the bona fide data of one dataset does not transfer to the other. Index TermsBiometrics, Face Image Quality Assessment, I. Text:"Can Coding Agents Sustain Continual Evolution without Forgetting? Shangziqi Zhao1, Yitong Zhang1, Jia Li1, 1Tsinghua University Abstract Recently, harness self-evolution has emerged as an important paradigm for improving agent performance. Prior studies show that evolving the agent harness can effectively enhance agent capabilities without modifying the foundation models parameters. We find that existing approaches largely study agent evolution on single task. In practice, coding agents may need to handle multiple task types over time. Whether an agent harness can continuously evolve across multiple tasks without suffering from catastrophic forgetting remains an important open question. In this paper, we investigate this open question through comprehensive empirical study of continual harness self-evolution. We organize three software engineering task datasets into six different evolution directions and apply two representative harness evolution methods to perform continual evolution along these directions. Our experimental results show that evolution on subsequent dataset can substantially undermine the performance on previously seen datasets. For example, with DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731, Meta-Harness improves its performance on SWE-Bench to 94.0% after evolution. However, after further evolution on FeatureBench, its performance on SWE-Bench drops to 32.0% by the fifth round, corresponding to 66% relative decrease. Finally, based on our experimental findings, we identify several insights, providing directions for the continual self-evolution of agent harnesses. Code is available at https://github.com/THU-Agent/continual-harness-evolution. Abstract Wave Forcing enables efficient continuous long-video generation with consistent appearance and natural motion through co-designed generation, training, and execution, achieving high throughput and quality.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Real-time long-video generation requires continuous output with consistent appearance and natural motion. Existing chunk-wise autoregressive methods face challenges in both generation efficiency and long-video quality: serialized denoising and history-cache refresh require two forward passes even with one-step denoising, and longer rollouts can degrade quality and cause camera drift. We propose Wave Forcing (WF), which co-designs generation, training, and execution for continuous video output with multi-step denoising. WF uses chunk-wise causal attention to access earlier, cleaner chunks before their denoising is complete, and distributes staggered denoising and cache-refresh stages across full-model replicas to achieve 1F1C: one output chunk per concurrent forward round after pipeline fill. To adapt the model to this continuous generation process, we develop a three-stage training procedure that successively introduces mixed-noise denoising, motion supervision, and causal adaptation using self-generated history. To execute this process efficiently, Wave Runtime (WaveRT) uses Wave Parallelism (WP) for early K/V delivery and communication-computation overlap, together with optimized kernels and pipelined VAE decoding. Experiments on Wan2.1-T2V-1.3B show that 5-step WF maintains overall quality comparable to Rolling Forcing while achieving higher Dynamic Degree on both short and long videos. Our system efficiency experiments show that 4-step WF achieves 147.6 DiT FPS and 134.8 end-to-end FPS on eight H200 GPUs, yielding 7.07x the single-GPU end-to-end throughput. In DiT-only tests with matched SageAttention+W8A8 backends, denoising steps, and GPU counts, WP delivers up to 2.53x the throughput of the faster of the Ulysses and Ring context-parallel baselines across six model scales (1.3B-14B).
[03.11.2026 11:38] Response: {"desc": "Статья предлагает метод Wave Forcing для непрерывной генерации длинных видео с постоянным внешним видом и естественным движением. Подход использует чанковое каузальное внимание (chunk-wise causal attention), которое позволяет обращаться к более ранним и чистым фрагментам до завершения их денойзинга, и распределяет стадии денойзинга и обновления кэша между копиями модели, достигая режима 1F1C. Обучение проходит в три этапа: смешанный шумовой денойзинг, supervision по движению и каузальная адаптация на собственной истории генерации. Система WaveRT с Wave Parallelism и оптимизированными ядрами показывает до 7.07x пропускной способности на восьми GPU по сравнению с одним GPU и до 2.53x по сравнению с Ulysses и Ring.", "emoji": "🌊", "title": "Непрерывные длинные видео в реальном времени благодаря волновому конвейеру"}

Abstract Agent harnesses integrate video generation and world models to enable planning, tool use, and recursive self-improvement for autonomous multi-shot video production.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Video generation is shifting from end-to-end synthesis toward agentic generation, where planning, tool use, and feedback enable coherent, controllable multi-shot videos. In parallel, video world models provide action-conditioned observations and predicted futures, serving as interactive environments for agents. We study their intersection through the agent harness: an executable runtime that constructs context, exposes tools, maintains state, routes feedback, and manages budgets around video models. This report examines three directions. First, agentic video generation, where harnesses coordinate scripting, shot planning, asset management, production, and editing, alongside the evaluation challenges of these systems. Second, agentic world modeling, where harnesses mediate observation, control, execution, and state. We discuss hybrid designs in which game engines or agent-written programs maintain world state while video models render observations, and how agents and world models can mutually evaluate and improve one another. Third, recursive self-improvement (RSI) in video model development, distinguishing output revisions, updates to harness state, generator parameters, and evaluators, and changes to the improvement procedure itself. We identify how these improvements feed back into subsequent development. Across these directions, we analyze essential agent capabilities, including audiovisual perception, long-horizon planning, memory, code-based interaction, feedback-guided control, skill reuse, and multi-agent coordination. We conclude with challenges in autonomous video production, lifelong memory, programmable hybrid control, autonomous model development, provenance, cost accounting, and evaluation linking video quality and task performance to sustained improvements in models and harnesses.

Abstract CoworkOS is a long-horizon agent harness that separates strategy from execution, supports human-in-the-loop collaboration via shared artifacts, and improves model performance through sustained automated post-training.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Rapid advances in cowork agents are shifting human-AI collaboration toward real-world automation, delivering software, designs, and other artifacts. However, such productivity work demands both high-quality delivery on complex productivity tasks and continued monitoring and decision-making over long horizons, a combination that remains an open challenge. We introduce CoworkOS, a general-purpose harness for long-horizon work that separates task strategy from execution management: the model decides actions, delegation, and acceptance, while the runtime maintains state and feedback. Its three-layer architecture enables direct execution or delegation to Subagents, synchronously or asynchronously, under stable responsibilities. Through a shared application layer, humans and agents inspect and revise the same evolving documents, presentations, CAD models, and Blender scenes, so human-in-the-loop participation can enter both through conversation and through direct modification of the work itself. To sustain continuity across agents and sessions, CoworkOS further introduces hierarchical workspaces and persistent semantic memory, so working files, key decisions, and long-running Job logs remain shareable, reusable, and resumable after interruption. Across 12 benchmarks and 6 categories of real-world applications, CoworkOS proves highly competitive with frontier agent applications such as Codex and DeepSeek Harness. As a sustained-work case study, CoworkOS uses GLM-5.3 as its backbone to conduct RSI-driven automated post-training on Qwen3.6-35B-A3B. Over five rounds, the model's average score across eight benchmarks rose from 33.19 to 42.95 (+9.76). Looking ahead, we believe CoworkOS opens a meaningful path toward general cowork-agent intelligence.

Abstract CUA-Master uses nested recursive loops with open-world data and feedback-driven retries to continuously improve computer-use agents across diverse benchmarks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Despite recent advances in CUAs, their development remains constrained by the limited diversity, controllability, and reliability of interaction data. Moreover, conventional optimization pipelines lack iterative feedback, while existing recursive improvement approaches often rely on self-generated experiences, limiting ability expansion and yielding diminishing returns. To address these challenges, we introduce CUA-Master, a nested double-loop recursive improvement framework. At the system level, an outer world-data-model flywheel continuously incorporates open-world corpus and leverages evolving model weaknesses to guide subsequent data construction and optimization iterations. At the data level, an inner flywheel couples open-world seeding and autocatalytic propagation. The former mines authentic workflows as seed tasks from instructional videos, and employs visual-semantic dual-path trajectory reconstruction; The latter transforms these seeds into reusable primitives for controllable synthesis of diverse, complex, and verifiable tasks. To enable efficient and reliable trajectory collection, we further introduce a feedback-guided snapshot-rollback retry mechanism in various real environments. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CUA-Master-27B achieves competitive performance across GUI-only and GUI-CLI hybrid benchmarks, scoring 80.2 on OSWorld Verified, 36.9 on OSWorld 2.0, 43.9 on WeaveBench, and 25.0 on Agents' Last Exam. Our framework also substantially improves the quality and efficiency of data synthesis, and exhibits continuous gains across recursive iterations.

Abstract NorthCUA scales computer-use agents via an experience flywheel, recursive self-improvement, staged learning, and reinforcement learning to achieve state-of-the-art open-source GUI and web interaction results.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Scaling computer-use agents from individual actions to complex software work requires experience that connects decisions to complete outcomes. We present NorthCUA, an integrated system for turbocharging computer-use agents through experience at scale. Its Experience Flywheel supplies diverse executable tasks to the NorthCUA Harness, which sustains long interactions, records decisions, and checks task outcomes. The Flywheel uses these records to curate verified trajectories. For unsolved tasks, recursive self-improvement evolves reusable skills and preserves verified milestones so later attempts build on earlier progress. Staged Learning combines this experience with grounding data from the Flywheel to develop GUI competence through mid-training and long-horizon supervised fine-tuning. The policy then learns from fresh attempts in the same Harness through joint desktop-web reinforcement learning, closing the loop between experience production and policy improvement. The resulting Nano, Mini, and Pro family achieves state-of-the-art results among open-source models across GUI grounding, desktop execution, and Web interaction under the reported evaluation settings. For more responsive Web workflows, NorthCUA-Ultrafast pairs direct action selection with open-ended generation on pre-RL Nano, offering a measured quality-latency tradeoff. Together, these advances establish NorthCUA as a high-performing model family and a reusable foundation for agents that carry complex software work from intent to completion.

Abstract ReTrace is a framework that evaluates recovery behavior in computer-use agents by formalizing transition-consistent recovery and demonstrating that autonomous model-based recovery improves long-horizon reliability.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Recent advances in large (vision) language models have enabled computer-use agents that automate digital workflows via fine-grained keyboard and mouse operations. However, such agents remain fragile in long-horizon settings, where errors accumulate and executed actions leave unintended side effects, making the ability to recover from failures critical. Yet, existing remedies remain inadequate: environment resets are infeasible in proprietary settings, action replay is prohibitively expensive over long horizons, and API-based rollback is limited to web environments, leaving model-based recovery largely unexplored. To address this gap, we present ReTrace, a scalable evaluation framework for systematically studying recovery behavior in computer-use agents. We formalize Transition-Consistent Recovery: identical downstream actions from the recovered and target states must yield identical outcomes. We operationalize this criterion through goal-oriented action replay following controlled error injection. Using ReTrace, we present the first systematic study of autonomous, model-based recovery, revealing that (i) ideal recovery can unlock model's latent task completion capabilities, (ii) current computer-use agents fail to exploit their latent recovery capabilities, and (iii) accurate model-based recovery yields measurable end-to-end gains. Together, these findings establish recovery as a fundamental capability for building reliable computer-use agents. Our code and dataset is available at https://github.com/BangProx/ReTRACE.
[03.11.2026 11:39] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлен фреймворк ReTrace для оценки способности computer-use agents, то есть LLM-агентов, управляющих компьютером через клавиатуру и мышь, восстанавливаться после ошибок. Авторы формализуют критерий Transition-Consistent Recovery: одинаковые последующие действия из восстановленного и целевого состояний должны давать одинаковый результат. Оценка проводится через goal-oriented action replay после контролируемого внесения ошибок, что делает возможным систематическое изучение восстановления без сброса среды. Исследование показывает, что идеальное восстановление раскрывает скрытые возможности модели, текущие агенты плохо используют эти способности, а точное model-based восстановление даёт измеримый прирост в длинных задачах.", "emoji": "🔄", "title": "Умение восстанавливаться после ошибок — ключ к надёжным AI-агентам"}

Paper text to classify:

"Abstract ReTrace is a framework that evaluates recovery behavior in computer-use agents by formalizing transition-consistent recovery and demonstrating that autonomous model-based recovery improves long-horizon reliability.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Recent advances in large (vision) language models have enabled computer-use agents that automate digital workflows via fine-grained keyboard and mouse operations. However, such agents remain fragile in long-horizon settings, where errors accumulate and executed actions leave unintended side effects, making the ability to recover from failures critical. Yet, existing remedies remain inadequate: environment resets are infeasible in proprietary settings, action replay is prohibitively expensive over long horizons, and API-based rollback is limited to web environments, leaving model-based recovery largely unexplored. To address this gap, we present ReTrace, a scalable evaluation framework for systematically studying recovery behavior in computer-use agents. We formalize Transition-Consistent Recovery: identical downstream actions from the recovered and target states must yield identical outcomes. We operationalize this criterion through goal-oriented action replay following controlled error injection. Using ReTrace, we present the first systematic study of autonomous, model-based recovery, revealing that (i) ideal recovery can unlock model's latent task completion capabilities, (ii) current computer-use agents fail to exploit their latent recovery capabilities, and (iii) accurate model-based recovery yields measurable end-to-end gains. Together, these findings establish recovery as a fundamental capability for building reliable computer-use agents. Our code and dataset is available at https://github.com/BangProx/ReTRACE."

Abstract Reviso is an agentic video captioning framework that learns to prioritize key visual evidence through hierarchical planning and active perception, improving caption quality and downstream video understanding.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can generate detailed video captions, yet they may overemphasize minor details while overlooking essential context and key events. We argue that not all visual evidence deserves equal attention, as the importance of visual information depends on the content of each video. Motivated by this observation, we propose Reviso, an agentic video captioning framework that learns what to observe and how to gather relevant visual evidence. From a low-frame-rate global preview, Reviso constructs a hierarchical Context--Focus--Points (CFP) observation plan that identifies the video context, prioritizes key aspects, and defines concrete observation targets and corresponding tools. Guided by this plan, Reviso performs goal-directed active perception by selectively revisiting temporal segments and combining tools for fine-grained inspection. It interprets the acquired evidence, updates its plan as needed, and integrates global and local information to generate the final caption. To learn this observation strategy, we develop a verification-guided pipeline that constructs high-quality training trajectories through factual verification and teacher-guided refinement. We further train Reviso using Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), incorporating a key-fact reward for caption quality, a Plan Alignment Reward (PAR) for identifying important observation targets, and an Unproductive Tool-Call Penalty (UTCP) that adjusts observation-cost penalties based on caption quality. Experiments across four video captioning benchmarks show that Reviso outperforms the evaluated open-source baselines, while its generated captions better support downstream video question answering. These findings highlight the effectiveness and potential of priority-guided active observation for video captioning.
[03.11.2026 11:39] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлен Reviso — агентный фреймворк для описания видео, который учится определять, какие визуальные доказательства важнее всего. Сначала по низкочастотному глобальному обзору строится иерархический план Context–Focus–Points, после чего модель целенаправленно переосматривает нужные временные сегменты с помощью инструментов. Обучение выполняется на верифицированных траекториях с использованием GRPO и наград за качество описания, соответствие плану (PAR) и штрафа за непродуктивные вызовы инструментов (UTCP). Эксперименты на четырёх бенчмарках показывают превосходство над открытыми baseline-моделями и лучшую поддержку downstream-задачи video QA.", "emoji": "🎬", "title": "Смотри не всё подряд, а то, что важно: приоритетное активное наблюдение для описания видео"}

Abstract This report proposes a unified taxonomy and literature system for agentic computer graphics driven by large language models, analyzing trends and open challenges in human-agent collaboration and long-horizon planning.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Computer graphics is built on code. Large language model (LLM) agents can now generate graphics code and iteratively refine it through various feedback. This capability opens the door to a potentially new paradigm of computer graphics, with graphics algorithms increasingly built around agentic systems to automate progressively complex tasks. Over the past years, research on agentic graphics has expanded rapidly across a diverse range of tasks. This rapid growth makes it increasingly difficult for manual literature reviews to keep pace with new developments. At the same time, similar ideas are often described using different terminology across these tasks, further complicating efforts to develop a systematic understanding of the field. In this report, we address these challenges through two contributions. First, we introduce a unified taxonomy for characterizing agentic systems across diverse computer graphics tasks. Second, we develop an agentic literature system that can assist human researchers in analyzing existing and newly published papers at scale. Building on these contributions, we analyze emerging research trends and discuss open challenges in coordinating agents and tools across graphics tasks, designing interfaces and representations for human-agent collaboration, sustaining long-horizon planning and refinement, and reliably evaluating interactive graphics systems.

Abstract CrossSWE introduces a multi-repository benchmark showing that current LLMs struggle with cross-repository consistency during joint software development.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small As coding agents achieve increasingly high success rates on software engineering benchmarks, recent efforts have sought to increase task complexity through longer horizons and more extensive code modifications. However, real-world software development often spans multiple interdependent repositories, introducing additional challenges in dependency reasoning, change propagation, and interface consistency. Rather than further increasing complexity within individual repositories, we introduce CrossSWE, a benchmark for cross-repository joint development comprising 171 real-world instances spanning 165 repositories, 20 technical domains, and 13 programming languages. CrossSWE verifies the necessity of coordinated changes by testing whether partial repository modifications are insufficient to achieve the intended behavior. Our evaluation of five LLMs reveals substantial challenges, with the strongest model, GPT-6.1 Sol, achieving only 58.48% Pass@1. Furthermore, cross-repository inconsistencies emerge as the most prevalent failure mode (44.2%), highlighting the difficulty of maintaining consistency across interdependent repositories.

Abstract PhotoGuider selects viewing directions and frames 360° panoramas using shared composition-aware tokens and produces camera-parameter outputs with explanation-conditioned scoring.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Traditional image cropping assumes the photograph already contains the composition worth keeping, and asks only where to crop. A full 360^{circ} scene offers no such guarantee: the viewing direction in a scene has to be discovered before anything can be framed. We present PhotoGuider, a measure-then-frame method that answers both questions with compact learned scorers over one shared set of composition-aware features. The designed features are extracted using foundation and task-specific vision models and structured into four photographic tokens: Subject, Composition, Aesthetics, and Integrity. Our MLLM explainer is fine-tuned to generate composition-related explanations conditioned on images and measured token assessments. To our knowledge, PhotoGuider is the first method to combine explicit composition-based direction selection in 360^{circ} panoramas with refined framing, camera-parameter output for native re-rendering, and measurement-conditioned explanations. On two standard 2D cropping benchmarks, PhotoGuider achieves the highest average mIoU performance against ground-truth crops. In a blind ranking study, 120 participants ranked three cropping methods over 100 panoramic scenes from the public Poly Haven HDRI library, placing our framings first in 53.2% of cases, against 27.3% and 19.5% for two strong alternatives. Project page: https://ntuneillee.github.io/research/photoguider/
[03.11.2026 11:39] Response: {"desc": "PhotoGuider — это метод автоматического кадрирования 360°-панорам, который сначала находит наиболее удачное направление взгляда, а затем подбирает кадр. Он использует компактные обученные модули оценки над общим набором признаков, учитывающих композицию, которые разбиты на четыре токена: Subject, Composition, Aesthetics и Integrity. Для генерации объяснений применяется дообученная MLLM, которая опирается на изображения и измеренные оценки токенов. По результатам на двух стандартных бенчмарках и слепом исследовании с участием пользователей метод показывает лучшие результаты по mIoU и предпочтения людей.", "emoji": "📸", "title": "Сначала найди взгляд, потом кадрируй: композиция в 360°"}

Abstract  Personal AI agents are increasingly capable of handling long-horizon tasks across applications, yet users often need to invest substantial effort in specifying goals, resolving ambiguity, and verifying outcomes. We argue that progress should be measured by how effectively agents amplify human agency: enabling people to pursue their goals with less routine supervision while retaining control over consequential decisions. We advocate Avatar Intelligence, a paradigm of persistent personal agents that represent users' goals, values, and constraints through sustained decision delegation across contexts and time. We describe a maturity roadmap from human-directed execution to autonomous operation within delegated domains, and introduce the Agentic Oversight Index (AOI) as a framework for evaluating delivered value against agent-side operational cost and human oversight burden. We identify three core research challenges: identity-continuous personalization that carries user preferences across tasks, user-situated evolution through Rehearsal Engineering, and safety mechanisms that combine technical control with accountability and recourse. These directions define a research agenda for reliable decision delegation to personal agents across tasks and over time.

Abstract FLAMIA accelerates Transformer attention on NVIDIA Blackwell GPUs via MXFP8 and mixed-precision kernels that reduce quantization overhead, prevent causal leakage, and support variable-length packed documents.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Attention remains a substantial computational cost in Transformer training, particularly at long context lengths. NVIDIA Blackwell GPUs provide high-throughput low-precision matrix multiplication, but exploiting it in attention requires addressing quantization overhead, causal information leakage through shared scales, and numerical inconsistencies between the forward and backward passes. We present FLAMIA, a family of attention kernels for Blackwell with MXFP8 and mixed NVFP4/FP8 training paths. Our design combines token-block scaling that reuses quantized operands across matrix orientations, targeted safeguards against scale-induced leakage, and consistent forward and backward computations. Fused quantization and architecture-specific kernel optimizations reduce overhead, while document-local scaling and workload-aware scheduling support variable-length documents directly in packed layouts without repacking. Across various causal packed-document workloads, the MXFP8 path accelerates the combined forward and backward attention call, including quantization, by factors of 1.10--1.40times on B200 and 1.06--1.42times on B300 relative to the fastest evaluated baseline for each workload. We validate training stability and quality preservation on pretraining Llama-style models. These results show how jointly designing quantization, training numerics, and document scheduling translates low-bit hardware throughput into practical attention acceleration.

Авторы решают три проблемы: накладные расходы на квантизацию, утечку каузальной информации через общие scale-коэффициенты и численные расхождения между forward и backward проходами. "Abstract FLAMIA accelerates Transformer attention on NVIDIA Blackwell GPUs via MXFP8 and mixed-precision kernels that reduce quantization overhead, prevent causal leakage, and support variable-length packed documents.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Attention remains a substantial computational cost in Transformer training, particularly at long context lengths. We present FLAMIA, a family of attention kernels for Blackwell with MXFP8 and mixed NVFP4/FP8 training paths. Abstract WorldFlow is a generative world model that jointly predicts future video and 3D trajectories using continuous displacement tokens and a Mixture-of-Transformers backbone, improving physical dynamics prediction across diverse benchmarks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Predicting future physical states stands as a central challenge in embodied intelligence. Existing video world models leave 3D dynamics implicit, action-based formulations remain tied to specific robot configurations, and 3D motion prediction models use flow-only supervision, providing a limited learning signal for physical dynamics. To circumvent these limitations, we introduce , a generative world model that jointly predicts future video and query-based 3D trajectories. By parameterizing 3D motion as continuous displacement tokens aligned with the video grid, a unified Mixture-of-Transformers (MoT) backbone jointly denoises both modalities under a rectified-flow objective. This formulation couples explicit geometric dynamics with visual evolution, effectively unlocking the physical commonsense embedded within pretrained video models. To empirically scale and validate this unified architecture, we train  on a heterogeneous mixture of 1.44 million object-level samples spanning simulated and real-world domains. We further introduce WorldFlowBench, comprising 1,678 evaluation samples from 11 data sources across simulated and real-world interactions, including spatial reasoning tasks. Across all three evaluation sets, WorldFlow achieves the lowest average displacement error (ADE) and final displacement error (FDE) among the evaluated learning-based methods. Notably, on the challenging SpatialCog set, WorldFlow achieves an ADE of 0.060 m, a 54.2\% reduction compared with the strongest external baseline. It also reduces ADE and FDE by 55.9\% and 68.5\%, respectively, compared with its flow-only variant. Project page: https://worldflow-rbs.github.io/.

Модель обучена на 1,44 млн разнородных образцов из симуляций и реального мира и оценена на новом бенчмарке WorldFlowBench. WorldFlow достигает наименьших ADE и FDE среди learning-based методов, снижая ADE на 54,2% на SpatialCog по сравнению с лучшим внешним baseline.", "emoji": "🎬", "title": "Видео и 3D-движение предсказываются вместе, физика становится точнее"}

Abstract BYOD converts autoregressive language models into diffusion models via parameter-efficient LoRA adapters, enabling parallel generation with minimal training cost while preserving base-model knowledge.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Unlike autoregressive (AR) models, diffusion language models (DLMs) permit parallel token updates, flexible generation, and bidirectional attention, but leading systems rely on costly pretraining or full-model fine-tuning. We present Build Your Own DLM (BYOD), a generalizable LoRA-only recipe for parameter-efficient AR-to-DLM conversion. Without architecture-specific tuning, BYOD induces diffusion generation in Gemma 2 9B, Llama 3.1 8B, Qwen2.5 7B, and Ministral 8B; to our knowledge, this is the first reported AR-to-DLM conversion of a Mistral model. The adapters comprise 4.1-5.8% of final parameters while every base-model parameter remains frozen; each 25,000-iteration conversion needs at most 102.4M tokens, processed in 3.1-4.0 hours on one Google Colab GPU. All four models generate from fully masked sequences, use bidirectional context in a controlled attention test, and retain substantial parent-model knowledge. BYOD-Gemma is the strongest BYOD conversion on our nine-task evaluation, reaching 50.6% average accuracy compared with 51.9% for LLaDA 8B Instruct in the same harness. Open-generation quality at low network function evaluation (NFE) budgets remains the principal limitation: BYOD perplexity is 8.3-11.1 versus 4.1 for LLaDA and 2.2-3.0 for the AR parents when generating 128 tokens in 64 steps. To enable further experimentation and inspection, we provide a standalone training and evaluation implementation and interactive demos for all four conversions. BYOD thus provides a practical route for extending diffusion generation to other existing model families while identifying the gap that efficient conversion must next close.

Базовые параметры остаются замороженными, а адаптеры составляют около 4–6% от итоговых параметров, что делает конвертацию дешёвой: около 25 000 итераций на одном GPU за несколько часов. Полученные модели генерируют текст из полностью замаскированных последовательностей, используют двунаправленный контекст и сохраняют знания исходной модели, показывая качество, сопоставимое с LLaDA 8B Instruct на ряде задач. Abstract The Collaborative Intelligence Benchmark evaluates AI agents as teammates across 240 tasks, revealing that even top models have significant collaboration weaknesses unrelated to individual capability.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Progress in AI is often measured by what a single model can do on its own. Yet teams of AI agents are starting to make breakthroughs and discoveries in science, medicine, mathematics and software engineering. As AI begins to work in teams of agents and people, the ability to collaborate becomes a core part of their intelligence. Research on human teams has measured this ability for decades. Inspired by this, we introduce the Collaborative Intelligence Benchmark, which measures how well an AI agent works as a teammate. It contains 240 tasks across 8 dimensions of teamwork, from refusing an unsafe shortcut to checking the work that a weaker model leaves behind. Each task comes from a real record or an existing benchmark, from aviation accident reports and clinical cases to coding tasks and negotiations, and has a programmatically verifiable outcome. To isolate teamwork from individual capability, models are scored only on tasks that they can solve by themselves when they have full information. We evaluate 18 closed- and open-weight models and find that every model, across sizes and families, has clear weaknesses as a teammate, with the best model scoring only 82.2 out of 100. Frontier models almost always refuse unsafe shortcuts and report misconduct, yet they cave under interpersonal pressure, reaching the best overall deal in only 14% of conflicts, and their pass rate drops from 97% to 81% when they take over a task in which a weaker model has cheated. Crucially, general capability does not guarantee good teamwork, as models with nearly the same capability score differ by up to 11 points as teammates. "Abstract The Collaborative Intelligence Benchmark evaluates AI agents as teammates across 240 tasks, revealing that even top models have significant collaboration weaknesses unrelated to individual capability.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Progress in AI is often measured by what a single model can do on its own. Yet teams of AI agents are starting to make breakthroughs and discoveries in science, medicine, mathematics and software engineering. As AI begins to work in teams of agents and people, the ability to collaborate becomes a core part of their intelligence. Research on human teams has measured this ability for decades. Inspired by this, we introduce the Collaborative Intelligence Benchmark, which measures how well an AI agent works as a teammate. It contains 240 tasks across 8 dimensions of teamwork, from refusing an unsafe shortcut to checking the work that a weaker model leaves behind. Each task comes from a real record or an existing benchmark, from aviation accident reports and clinical cases to coding tasks and negotiations, and has a programmatically verifiable outcome. To isolate teamwork from individual capability, models are scored only on tasks that they can solve by themselves when they have full information. We evaluate 18 closed- and open-weight models and find that every model, across sizes and families, has clear weaknesses as a teammate, with the best model scoring only 82.2 out of 100. Frontier models almost always refuse unsafe shortcuts and report misconduct, yet they cave under interpersonal pressure, reaching the best overall deal in only 14% of conflicts, and their pass rate drops from 97% to 81% when they take over a task in which a weaker model has cheated. Crucially, general capability does not guarantee good teamwork, as models with nearly the same capability score differ by up to 11 points as teammates. The benchmark includes 160 public tasks with code and data, along with 80 held-out tasks for evaluation. As AI agents increasingly work alongside other agents and people, the ability to collaborate effectively is becoming a crucial capability to measure and develop."

Abstract TinyAstral improves robot agent harnesses via recursive self-improvement and distills compact students that retain performance gains with lower compute.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Robot agents built on vision-language models depend on both the planner and its harness: the tools, feedback, memory, and recovery logic around it. Can improvements to a large teacher's harness be carried into a compact agent with lower inference compute cost? We present TinyAstral, a two-stage approach. We first freeze an open-weight teacher and improve its harness through recursive self-improvement (RSI): frontier LLM agents read the teacher's trajectories and propose harness edits, and each edit is accepted only if it passes a paired statistical test. We then freeze the final harness, collect teacher trajectories with reasoning and failure recovery, and distill a small student that replaces the teacher in the same harness. On RoboTwin, harness evolution raises the teacher's success from 33.4% to 63.6%; the final 4B student reaches 62.8%. Across four harness versions, distilled 4B students score close to their corresponding teachers, and later versions yield stronger students, showing that harness evolution gains carry into compact models. The harness gains also transfer to other frozen planners without retraining. On identical episodes, the final 4B student outperforms open 4B agents, the open 27B harness-RSI model SenseNova-RoboRSI, and our own teacher model placed in three competitors' harnesses. It stays close to its teacher on all three training benchmarks, and on three benchmarks never used for RSI or training it scores close to a 552B planner running the same harness. On the measured deployment episodes, it uses about one fifth of its teacher's accelerator time.

Сначала авторы применяют рекурсивное самоулучшение (RSI), где LLM-агенты предлагают правки harness, а каждая правка принимается только после парного статистического теста. Затем финальный harness используется для сбора траекторий учителя и дистилляции маленького студента (4B), который почти не теряет в успешности, но использует примерно пятую часть вычислительных ресурсов. Abstract Lab2Sim introduces a benchmark for converting science videos into interactive virtual experiments, and EduVideo2Code improves apparatus fidelity by encoding procedures as state machines for verified code generation.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Science experiment videos demonstrate how experiments are performed, but offer no direct interaction for learners. Turning them into virtual experiments requires not only reproducing the observed apparatus and state changes, but also enforcing the preconditions governing each operation. We introduce Lab2Sim, a benchmark of 218 science experiment videos with evidence-grounded specifications and 11,881 rubric items. To address this task, we propose EduVideo2Code, which extracts experimental procedures hierarchically, encodes them as explicit state machines to guide code generation, and refines the resulting pages through execution-based verification. Experiments show that existing multimodal models readily generate runnable pages but struggle to faithfully reconstruct experimental apparatus. EduVideo2Code consistently improves all four evaluation dimensions across two backbone models, with apparatus fidelity gains of 24.15 points.
[03.11.2026 11:40] Response: {"desc": "Авторы представляют Lab2Sim — бенчмарк из 218 видео научных экспериментов с 11 881 пунктами оценки, предназначенный для превращения видео в интерактивные виртуальные лабораторные работы. Метод EduVideo2Code иерархически извлекает процедуры эксперимента и кодирует их в виде явных state machines, которые направляют генерацию кода. Затем сгенерированные страницы уточняются через execution-based verification, что повышает корректность выполнения. "Abstract Lab2Sim introduces a benchmark for converting science videos into interactive virtual experiments, and EduVideo2Code improves apparatus fidelity by encoding procedures as state machines for verified code generation.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Science experiment videos demonstrate how experiments are performed, but offer no direct interaction for learners. Turning them into virtual experiments requires not only reproducing the observed apparatus and state changes, but also enforcing the preconditions governing each operation. We introduce Lab2Sim, a benchmark of 218 science experiment videos with evidence-grounded specifications and 11,881 rubric items. To address this task, we propose EduVideo2Code, which extracts experimental procedures hierarchically, encodes them as explicit state machines to guide code generation, and refines the resulting pages through execution-based verification. Experiments show that existing multimodal models readily generate runnable pages but struggle to faithfully reconstruct experimental apparatus. EduVideo2Code consistently improves all four evaluation dimensions across two backbone models, with apparatus fidelity gains of 24.15 points."

Abstract Library of Layers dynamically orders transformer blocks during inference to adapt computation to tasks, enabling parameter reuse, sparsity, and interpretable routing across reasoning, multilingual, and safety settings.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Computer programmers chain together the same reusable computational primitives, for example, conditional statements or loops, but in different orders to implement different algorithms. In contrast, existing language model architectures always execute their layers in the same order. We introduce Library of Layers (LoL), an architecture that dynamically orders its layers during inference to solve the problem at hand. LoL has a single hyperparameter, the size of its global library of transformer blocks, that allows it to transition seamlessly between parameter reuse and sparsity, generalizing the parameter-efficiency and FLOPs-efficiency tradeoffs of dense, depth-recurrent, and mixture of experts architectures in one unified family. We show that LoL achieves competitive performance with all three above baselines when pretrained on text data. On synthetic sudoku and maze data, we observe that the model employs distinct layer orderings to implement different solution strategies, and we can intervene on routing to force a model to apply a specific solution strategy to a problem instance. In a multilingual natural language setting, we find that LoL chooses different layer order patterns based on language and script. Similarly, we train LoL on safety data and fit probes to its router outputs, finding that these probes distinguish safe from unsafe text with high AUROC. LoL provides a path toward a future in which models dynamically compose their own computational graph, enabling more efficient and controllable reasoning.
[03.11.2026 11:40] Response: {"desc": "Авторы представляют архитектуру Library of Layers (LoL), в которой модель динамически выбирает порядок применения трансформерных блоков из общей библиотеки во время inference. Единственный гиперпараметр, размер библиотеки, позволяет плавно переходить между повторным использованием параметров и разреженностью, объединяя dense, depth-recurrent и mixture of experts архитектуры. На задачах судоку, лабиринтов, многоязычного текста и безопасности LoL формирует разные порядки слоёв, а маршрутизатор можно интерпретировать и даже направлять вручную. Пробы (probes), обученные на выходах роутера, хорошо различают безопасный и небезопасный текст по метрике AUROC.", "emoji": "🧩", "title": "Модель сама собирает свой вычислительный граф под задачу"}

Abstract The study evaluates frozen video representations to show that prediction errors reflect appearance and motion rather than reliably measuring physical surprise.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Prediction errors from video world models are often treated as physical surprise, but they can also reflect appearance, static content, motion, or ordinary prediction difficulty. We present an evaluation framework that separates what frozen representations contain, how they organize appearance and physical differences, and what changes a model's own prediction error. On IntPhys2, a benchmark of physically possible and impossible events, we compare representations from latent prediction, masked pixel reconstruction, and simple visual statistics. Appearance remains easy to decode, while V-JEPA2 exposes modest information about whether events are possible or impossible. Looking at the latent geometry, appearance changes become smaller compared with differences between videos, their directions line up more consistently across scenes, and their overlap with physical changes decreases in deeper layers. Across action-conditioned models, incorrect actions raise prediction error, while appearance changes also produce substantial responses. In Physion++ video clips, repeating a single frame can produce higher error than the original clip, while changing color throughout the clip can lower it. Using Kubric, a simulation and rendering framework, we find that impossible events can produce lower error than possible events. A fixed error threshold also incorrectly flags many possible events as impossible. A model can therefore represent physical information and respond to incorrect actions without its native prediction error reliably measuring physical surprise.

Авторы сравнивают латентные предсказательные модели (например, V-JEPA2), реконструкцию замаскированных пикселей и простые визуальные статистики на бенчмарке IntPhys2 и других датасетах. Результаты показывают, что ошибка предсказания в основном чувствительна к внешнему виду, движению и сложности самого предсказания, а не к физической невозможности события. "Abstract The study evaluates frozen video representations to show that prediction errors reflect appearance and motion rather than reliably measuring physical surprise.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Prediction errors from video world models are often treated as physical surprise, but they can also reflect appearance, static content, motion, or ordinary prediction difficulty. We present an evaluation framework that separates what frozen representations contain, how they organize appearance and physical differences, and what changes a model's own prediction error. On IntPhys2, a benchmark of physically possible and impossible events, we compare representations from latent prediction, masked pixel reconstruction, and simple visual statistics. Appearance remains easy to decode, while V-JEPA2 exposes modest information about whether events are possible or impossible. Looking at the latent geometry, appearance changes become smaller compared with differences between videos, their directions line up more consistently across scenes, and their overlap with physical changes decreases in deeper layers. Across action-conditioned models, incorrect actions raise prediction error, while appearance changes also produce substantial responses. In Physion++ video clips, repeating a single frame can produce higher error than the original clip, while changing color throughout the clip can lower it. Using Kubric, a simulation and rendering framework, we find that impossible events can produce lower error than possible events. A fixed error threshold also incorrectly flags many possible events as impossible. A model can therefore represent physical information and respond to incorrect actions without its native prediction error reliably measuring physical surprise."

Abstract Tutorial Agentic Learning enables LLM agents to continuously learn from instructional videos without parameter updates by building a structured tutorialbook memory and retrieving via reasoning-linked table-of-contents trees.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Humans continuously acquire knowledge from carefully crafted materials such as books and tutorial videos, carrying that understanding into future tasks. Millions of such instructional videos are uploaded online every day, yet LLM agents remain unable to exploit this ever-expanding wealth of human teaching. Their capabilities are frozen after training, while parameter updates at scale remain prohibitively expensive. This raises the central question: can LLM agents learn from this vast instructional resource without parameter updates? We introduce Tutorial Agentic Learning (TAL), a nonparametric continual-learning paradigm that consolidates tutorial videos into a persistent, structured tutorialbook memory through a two-stage adversarial process that harvests hard, video-grounded questions and uses them to refine the memory. At inference time, TAL retrieves through a Table-of-Contents (TOC) tree, tying retrieval directly to the model's reasoning rather than to embedding similarity. To study this setting, we introduce VidLearn, a multimodal QA benchmark built from 72 chess tutorial videos spanning 12 openings. On VidLearn, TAL outperforms all evaluated baselines and improves monotonically as more videos are consumed, achieving up to 113% and 91% relative gains over the corresponding vanilla MLLMs on the training and test splits, respectively. In actual gameplay, TAL reaches a stronger opening-phase position than its vanilla counterpart in 69% of head-to-head games. Evaluation in a second domain further demonstrates the generality of TAL. Together, these results highlight instructional videos as a scalable resource for parameter-update-free agent continual learning.

Видео консолидируются в структурированную память tutorialbook через двухэтапный адверсариальный процесс, который генерирует сложные вопросы и уточняет память. На этапе инференса поиск выполняется по дереву Table-of-Contents, связанному с логикой рассуждений модели, а не с embedding similarity. На новом бенчмарке VidLearn по шахматным видео TAL превосходит базовые MLLM до 113% и показывает стабильный рост качества по мере изучения новых видео.", "emoji": "🎓", "title": "Агенты учатся на видеоуроках без переобучения модели"}

Abstract SIMIT is a test-time framework that lets vision-language models generate their own practice data to improve answers on unlabeled queries without external annotations.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small We introduce SIMIT, a test-time self-improvement framework in which a vision-language model creates its own query-specific training data before answering an unlabeled test query. Our goal is to improve performance on a given target test set without annotations or external models. To pursue this goal, some test-time methods improve answers through longer reasoning or repeated sampling but leave the model unchanged. Others update the model using rewards based on majority agreement among multiple candidate answers or the model's own judgments of correctness, but such rewards can reinforce existing mistakes. SIMIT instead imagines similar problems whose solutions it controls: a model need not correctly solve the target query to construct useful practice examples. It synthesizes diverse (question, answer, image) triplets by specifying each answer before realizing its image. To support diverse multimodal tasks, SIMIT combines native image generation with an extensible skill library for structured visuals, including documents, charts, and diagrams. Within this pipeline, multi-stage verification checks that images support their assigned answers, while difficulty filtering retains informative examples. Adaptive budgeting further targets this practice by allocating more samples to queries the model is less confident in answering. The resulting data can improve the model in-context (SIMIT-ICL) or in-weight (SIMIT-FT). Across 17 diverse benchmarks, SIMIT outperforms existing self-improvement methods. Its best configuration achieves a +7.20% mean relative gain over BAGEL-7B, while training-free SIMIT-ICL achieves +6.98%.
[03.11.2026 11:41] Response: {"desc": "SIMIT — это фреймворк для self-improvement на этапе тестирования, в котором vision-language model сама генерирует обучающие примеры под конкретный запрос без разметки и внешних моделей. Вместо того чтобы полагаться на majority voting или собственные оценки, которые могут закреплять ошибки, модель задаёт ответ заранее и затем синтезирует соответствующее изображение и вопрос как тройку (question, answer, image). Для разнообразия используются native image generation и библиотека навыков для структурированных визуалов, а multi-stage verification и difficulty filtering отбирают корректные и информативные примеры. Адаптивное распределение бюджета направляет больше сэмплов на запросы, в которых модель менее уверена, а полученные данные используются либо in-context (SIMIT-ICL), либо для fine-tuning весов (SIMIT-FT), давая в среднем +7.20% относительного прироста над BAGEL-7B на 17 бенчмарках.", "emoji": "🧠", "title": "Модель учится на примерах, которые создаёт сама, прямо во время теста"}
