Abstract An audio-guided framework restores facial details in AI-generated videos by combining reference-guided flow modeling with audio synchronization to resolve lip and dental ambiguity.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small AI-generated videos often depict people within detailed scenes while leaving their small facial regions poorly resolved. Visual super-resolution can sharpen these regions without resolving whether the recovered lips and teeth agree with the spoken audio. We present an audio-guided facial video restoration framework that addresses this ambiguity within a reference-guided video flow model. Source--noise mixing initializes reconstruction from a partially preserved observation, and structured reference injection separates appearance anchors from continuous motion context. Training combines restoration of degraded images and videos with correction of degraded dubbed sources under target audio. Appearance and synchronization supervision allow the model to recover facial detail and support visual dubbing when the audio is replaced. On talking-head restoration benchmarks, our method achieves the best paired perceptual similarity and synchronization scores among the compared restoration methods. Component comparisons examine audio conditioning, the reconstruction formulation, and reference injection. Native AIGC examples further illustrate the recovery of lip contours, dental structure, and surrounding facial texture in scene-level footage. injection. Native AIGC examples further illustrate the recovery of lip contours, dental structure, and surrounding facial texture in scene-level footage.

Метод основан на reference-guided video flow model, который использует смешивание источника и шума для инициализации реконструкции и структурированную инъекцию референсных изображений для разделения якорей внешнего вида и непрерывного контекста движения. Abstract Face image quality assessment methods vary widely in detecting diffusion-based and other morphing attacks, with performance depending heavily on attack type and dataset, and high quality scores for morphs often indicate poor detection.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Face image quality assessment (FIQA) estimates the utility of a face image for automated face recognition and has been proposed as a morphing attack detection (MAD) signal that requires no morph-specific training. We evaluate 17 FIQA methods from all current FIQA categories on 20 morphing attack sets from two datasets, FRLL and FEI, including five diffusion-based attack sets. Each quality score is used directly as a MAD decision score. Unlike prior work, which inverted a quality measure when this lowered its error, we fix the score direction of each method in advance from its definition, so that cases in which morphs receive higher quality than bona fide samples remain visible. On FRLL, DifFIQA(R) achieves a mean D-EER of 11.42%, and together with CLIB-FIQA and eDifFIQA(L) clearly outperforms all other FIQA methods. A strong separation of bona fide and morph scores does not imply good detection performance: on the diffusion-based attacks, up to 11 of 17 methods assign higher quality to morphs than to bona fide samples, and the largest reversed separation in the study coincides with the worst detection result of the study. Performance also depends strongly on the attack: GraFIQs(L), the best detector of DCMorph and Arc2Morph on FRLL, performs poorly on the landmark-based and GAN-based attacks. On FEI, the lowest mean D-EER is 39.75%, and a threshold set on the bona fide data of one dataset does not transfer to the other.

Авторы тестируют 17 методов FIQA на 20 наборах атак из датасетов FRLL и FEI, включая пять диффузионных атак, и используют оценки качества напрямую как решающий скор. Направление оценки каждого метода фиксируется заранее по его определению, поэтому случаи, когда морфы получают более высокое качество, чем подлинные образцы, остаются видимыми. Abstract Empirical study shows continual harness self-evolution across software engineering tasks causes severe catastrophic forgetting, with performance on earlier datasets dropping sharply after later evolution.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Recently, harness self-evolution has emerged as an important paradigm for improving agent performance. Prior studies show that evolving the agent harness can effectively enhance agent capabilities without modifying the foundation model's parameters. We find that existing approaches largely study agent evolution on a single task. In practice, coding agents may need to handle multiple task types over time. Whether an agent harness can continuously evolve across multiple tasks without suffering from catastrophic forgetting remains an important open question.   In this paper, we investigate this open question through a comprehensive empirical study of continual harness self-evolution. We organize three software engineering task datasets into six different evolution directions and apply two representative harness evolution methods to perform continual evolution along these directions. Our experimental results show that evolution on a subsequent dataset can substantially undermine the performance on previously seen datasets. For example, with DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731, Meta-Harness improves its performance on SWE-Bench to 94.0% after evolution. However, after further evolution on FeatureBench, its performance on SWE-Bench drops to 32.0% by the fifth round, corresponding to a 66% relative decrease. Finally, based on our experimental findings, we identify several insights, providing directions for the continual self-evolution of agent harnesses. Code is available at https://github.com/THU-Agent/continual-harness-evolution.

Prior studies show that evolving the agent harness can effectively enhance agent capabilities without modifying the foundation model's parameters. We find that existing approaches largely study agent evolution on a single task. In practice, coding agents may need to handle multiple task types over time. Whether an agent harness can continuously evolve across multiple tasks without suffering from catastrophic forgetting remains an important open question.   In this paper, we investigate this open question through a comprehensive empirical study of continual harness self-evolution. We organize three software engineering task datasets into six different evolution directions and apply two representative harness evolution methods to perform continual evolution along these directions. Our experimental results show that evolution on a subsequent dataset can substantially undermine the performance on previously seen datasets. For example, with DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731, Meta-Harness improves its performance on SWE-Bench to 94.0% after evolution. However, after further evolution on FeatureBench, its performance on SWE-Bench drops to 32.0% by the fifth round, corresponding to a 66% relative decrease.
